The core challenge is not retrieval but lifecycle management: determining which memories should persist, which should be replaced, and at what <sub>ra</sub>t<sub>e, con</sub>diti<sub>one</sub>d <sub>on</sub> th<sub>e</sub> b<sub>e</sub>h<sub>av</sub>i<sub>ora</sub>l t<sub>ype o</sub>f <sub>eac</sub>h f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>.</sub>

Buildin<sub>g</sub> on earl<sub>y</sub> work on LLM a<sub>g</sub>ent memor<sub>y</sub> [20], recent s<sub>y</sub>stems have established stron<sub>g</sub> infrastructure: tem<sub>p</sub>orall<sub>y</sub>-aware knowled<sub>g</sub>e <sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>hs (Ze<sub>p</sub>/Gra<sub>p</sub>hiti [2]), Zettelkasten or<sub>g</sub>anization (A-MEM [4]), OS-ins<sub>p</sub>ired hierarchies (Memor<sub>y</sub>OS [9]), RL-learned o<sub>p</sub>erations (Memor<sub>y</sub>-R1 [10]), and ada<sub>p</sub>tive structure selection (FluxMem [12]). None distin<sub>g</sub>uishes a current <sub>p</sub>reference from <sub>a superse</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d <sub>one, recogn</sub>i<sub>zes exp</sub>i<sub>re</sub>d l<sub>og</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>cs, or mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s an approac</sub>hi<sub>ng</sub> d<sub>ea</sub>dli<sub>ne, an</sub>d <sub>ex</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> benchmarks [1] likewise do not measure whether a s<sub>y</sub>stem can tell current from outdated state.

We introduce Fortunate Recall (FR), a composable lifecycle policy layer that classifies each extracted fact into one of 10+1 behavioral cate<sub>g</sub>ories (Table 1)—not co<sub>g</sub>nitive t<sub>yp</sub>es (e<sub>p</sub>isodic/semantic/<sub>p</sub>rocedural) but behavioral domains that directl<sub>y</sub> determine tem<sub>p</sub>oral d<sub>y</sub>namics— <sub>an</sub>d <sub>app</sub>li<sub>es</sub> <sub>ca</sub>t<sub>egory-spec</sub>ifi<sub>c</sub> d<sub>ecay,</sub> <sub>supersess</sub>i<sub>on,</sub> <sub>even</sub>t<sub>-</sub>ti<sub>me</sub> <sub>va</sub>lidit<sub>y,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>rou</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> <sub>po</sub>li<sub>c</sub>i<sub>es</sub> <sub>as</sub> d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>erm</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>c</sub> f<sub>unc</sub>ti<sub>ons.</sub> E<sub>very re</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>eva</sub>l d<sub>ec</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>on</sub> i<sub>s a name</sub>d t<sub>erm o</sub>f <sub>a s</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>l<sub>e c</sub>l<sub>ose</sub>d<sub>-</sub>f<sub>orm</sub> l<sub>og-score,</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>no</sub> l<sub>earne</sub>d bl<sub>ac</sub>k b<sub>ox me</sub>di<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e</sub> b<sub>e</sub>h<sub>av</sub>i<sub>or, a</sub>t <sub>a me</sub>di<sub>an</sub> 47 <sub>�s an</sub>d <sub>emp</sub>i<sub>r</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> li<sub>near</sub> � (�) scaling.

## 2 Related Work and Gap Analysis

Existin<sub>g</sub> s<sub>y</sub>stems exhibit com<sub>p</sub>lementar<sub>y</sub> <sub>g</sub>a<sub>p</sub>s, ma<sub>pp</sub>ed feature b<sub>y</sub> feature in Table 32 (A<sub>pp</sub>endix E); we summarize the <sub>p</sub>attern rather than enumerate it. Flat vector stores (Mem0 [19]) and d<sub>y</sub>namic or<sub>g</sub>anizers (A-MEM [4]) rovide no structured lifec cle. Co nitive-t e cate orizations (Memor OS [9], MIRIX [11]) describe memory form rather than behavior: a chronic illness and a lunch order are both “semantic” <sub>y</sub>et need ver<sub>y</sub> diferent d<sub>y</sub>namics. Memor<sub>y</sub>-R1 [10] learns o<sub>p</sub>erations b<sub>y</sub> RL over a flat {ADD, UPDATE, DELETE} space containing no expir<sub>y</sub> or retraction action, so its gap is vocabular<sub>y</sub> rather than o<sub>p</sub>timization. Memor<sub>y</sub>Bank [5] a<sub>pp</sub>lies Ebbin<sub>g</sub>haus [21] curves with one uniform deca<sub>y</sub> for all fact t<sub>yp</sub>es. MemGPT [8] and, at <sub>q</sub>uer<sub>y</sub> time, APEX-MEM [17] dele<sub>g</sub>ate lifec<sub>y</sub>cle d<sub>ec</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>ons</sub> t<sub>o</sub> LLM <sub>reason</sub>i<sub>ng—expens</sub>i<sub>ve</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>opaque</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> FR’<sub>s</sub> <sub>po</sub>li<sub>c</sub>i<sub>es</sub> <sub>are</sub> d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>erm</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>c—an</sub>d neither t<sub>yp</sub>es facts, a<sub>pp</sub>lies diferential deca<sub>y</sub>, or models event-time validit<sub>y</sub>. Ze<sub>p</sub>/Gra<sub>p</sub>hiti [2] ofers <sub>pro</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> t<sub>empora</sub>l k<sub>now</sub>l<sub>e</sub>d<sub>ge grap</sub>h<sub>s w</sub>ith <sub>no</sub> f<sub>orge</sub>tti<sub>ng mec</sub>h<sub>an</sub>i<sub>sm an</sub>d <sub>on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> bi<sub>nary supersess</sub>i<sub>on;</sub> FluxMem [12] adapts memory structure rather than policy. FR’s bi-temporal representation parallels the valid-time / transaction-time distinction in tem<sub>p</sub>oral databases [16], extended with behavioral <sub>con</sub>diti<sub>on</sub>i<sub>ng.</sub>

Benchmarks. LongMemEval [1] and LoCoMo [13] measure long-horizon retrieval without testing whether a s<sub>y</sub>stem distin<sub>g</sub>uishes current from outdated state, and BEAM [14] s<sub>p</sub>ans ten memor<sub>y</sub> <sub>a</sub>biliti<sub>es</sub> <sub>a</sub>t <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t<sub>s</sub> <sub>up</sub> t<sub>o</sub> 10M t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>ens,</sub> <sub>severa</sub>l <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>em</sub> lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e-sens</sub>iti<sub>ve.</sub> W<sub>e</sub> th<sub>ere</sub>f<sub>ore</sub> <sub>pos</sub>iti<sub>on</sub> LifecycleBench not as the first benchmark to touch temporal updates or forgetting but as a dense lifecycle stress test, every question constructed so the correct answer depends on lifecycle state. Since we introduce both benchmark and method, §6.6 transfers the same com<sub>p</sub>arison onto BEAM, which <sub>we</sub> did <sub>no</sub>t b<sub>u</sub>ild<sub>.</sub>

Figure 1: Fortunate Recall architecture and the LLM / lifecycle-math boundary. Ingestion (to<sub>p</sub>, as<sub>y</sub>nchronous): one gpt-4.1-mini call <sub>p</sub>er turn extracts each ed<sub>g</sub>e � and ta<sub>g</sub>s it with behavioral category �, slot key �, lifecycle state �, event-time anchor ℎ, ingest time �, and confidence �; � and ℎ are two independent clocks (bi-temporal). Retrieval (bottom, per query): hybrid candidate generation is distilled by a single small-model call to top-20, which the deterministic lifecycle layer ranks by the closed-form lo<sub>g</sub>-score of Eq. (2). The <sub>p</sub>i<sub>p</sub>eline uses exactl<sub>y</sub> two LLM calls <sub>p</sub>er turn, and ever<sub>y</sub> lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e</sub> d<sub>ec</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>on</sub> i<sub>s</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>name</sub>d t<sub>erm</sub> <sub>o</sub>f ℓ<sub>,</sub> i<sub>nspec</sub>t<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> <sub>per</sub> <sub>e</sub>d<sub>ge</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>per</sub> t<sub>erm</sub> <sub>a</sub>t <sub>m</sub>illi<sub>secon</sub>d l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ency.</sub>

Distinction from cognitive typologies. MemoryOS and MIRIX [11] categorize by cognitive type (episodic/semantic/procedural), which describes theform of memory. Our ontology describes the behavioral domain: a parent’s chronic illness is “semantic” in cognitive typology but “Health & W<sub>e</sub>llb<sub>e</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>” i<sub>n</sub> <sub>ours;</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>mee</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> ti<sub>me</sub> i<sub>s</sub> <sub>a</sub>l<sub>so</sub> “<sub>seman</sub>ti<sub>c</sub>” b<sub>u</sub>t “L<sub>og</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>ca</sub>l C<sub>on</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t<sub>.</sub>” Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>cogn</sub>iti<sub>ve</sub> t<sub>ype</sub> d<sub>oes</sub> <sub>no</sub>t d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>erm</sub>i<sub>ne</sub> <sub>appropr</sub>i<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e</sub> d<sub>ynam</sub>i<sub>cs;</sub> th<sub>e</sub> b<sub>e</sub>h<sub>av</sub>i<sub>ora</sub>l d<sub>oma</sub>i<sub>n</sub> d<sub>oes.</sub>

## 3.2 Classify the Fact, Not the Entity

Classification must target thefact (the relational edge), not the conversational utterance or the entity <sub>no</sub>d<sub>e.</sub> Utt<sub>erance-</sub>l<sub>eve</sub>l <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>on con</sub>f<sub>uses</sub> th<sub>e conversa</sub>ti<sub>ona</sub>l f<sub>rame w</sub>ith th<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>orma</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> b<sub>e</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> memorized (“I chan<sub>g</sub>ed m<sub>y</sub> last name while u<sub>p</sub>datin<sub>g</sub> m<sub>y</sub> insurance <sub>p</sub>a<sub>p</sub>erwork” is about Identit<sub>y</sub>, not Obli<sub>g</sub>ations). Entit<sub>y</sub>-level classification assi<sub>g</sub>ns wron<sub>g</sub> lifec<sub>y</sub>cle <sub>p</sub>olicies to ed<sub>g</sub>es that ha<sub>pp</sub>en to involve the same node (“Alex caught 7 bass” should be Hobbies, not Relational just because Alex is a <sub>p</sub>erson). Fact-level classification ensures each ed<sub>g</sub>e receives its cate<sub>g</sub>or<sub>y</sub> based on what it re<sub>p</sub>resents. A reproducible three-judge classifier-agreement audit $( \kappa = + 0 . 6 7$ with full session context) is in A<sub>ppen</sub>di<sub>x</sub> B<sub>.</sub>1<sub>.</sub>1<sub>.</sub>

## 3.3 Lifecycle Mechanisms

Diferential temporal decay. Each category has a base decay rate $\lambda _ { c }$ <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>ac</sub>ti<sub>va</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> $a ( e ) =$ $\exp ( - \lambda _ { c } \cdot \Delta t )$ . Rates s<sub>p</sub>an a 5.3× ran<sub>g</sub>e from Identit<sub>y</sub> (slowest) to Lo<sub>g</sub>istical (fastest), calibrated to <sub>p</sub>revent cate<sub>g</sub>or<sub>y</sub> hierarch<sub>y</sub> squattin<sub>g</sub> (A<sub>pp</sub>endix I).

Slot-key supersession with confidence weighting. For categories with replace semantics, normalized (subject, attribute) slot ke<sub>y</sub>s tri<sub>gg</sub>er su<sub>p</sub>ersession. Hi<sub>g</sub>h-confidence contradictions mark old ed<sub>g</sub>es inactive; low-confidence cases <sub>p</sub>reserve both values (soft su<sub>p</sub>ersession). “Thinkin<sub>g</sub> about movin<sub>g</sub> to London” <sub>p</sub>reserves “lives in Istanbul”; “moved to London” su<sub>p</sub>ersedes it (A<sub>pp</sub>endix F).

Event-time validity and anticipatory activation. For Obligations and Logistics, activation is governed by distance to the event, not from creation. Deadlines increase in activation as they approach, th<sub>en</sub> <sub>exp</sub>i<sub>re</sub> <sub>a</sub>ft<sub>er</sub> <sub>pass</sub>i<sub>ng.</sub> A d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e-aware</sub> filt<sub>er</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith b<sub>ac</sub>k<sub>war</sub>d<sub>-</sub>l<sub>oo</sub>ki<sub>ng</sub> b<sub>ypass</sub> <sub>removes</sub> <sub>pas</sub>t<sub>-</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>even</sub>t<sub>s</sub> b<sub>e</sub>f<sub>ore reran</sub>ki<sub>ng.</sub>

Category-aware retrieval routing. Query classification pulls per-category candidate sets alongside global semantic candidates. Category-forced retrieval extends this: for the predicted category, all ed<sub>g</sub>es are retrieved (u<sub>p</sub> to 20), b<sub>yp</sub>assin<sub>g</sub> vocabular<sub>y</sub> <sub>g</sub>a<sub>p</sub>s between abstract queries and s<sub>p</sub>ecific stored f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>s.</sub>

## 4 Theoretical Foundations

Representation-level impossibility, holding for any scorer in the stated feature <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub> <sub>regar</sub>dl<sub>ess</sub> <sub>o</sub>f t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng:</sub> Th<sub>eorem</sub> 1<sub>,</sub> Th<sub>eorem</sub> 3 <sub>w</sub>ith it<sub>s</sub> f<sub>our</sub> <sub>w</sub>it<sub>nesses,</sub> <sub>an</sub>d th<sub>e</sub> <sub>ac</sub>ti<sub>on-space</sub> ana<sup>l</sup>o<sub>g</sub>s ${ \overline { { 3 ^ { \prime } } } }$ <sub>an</sub>d $3 ^ { \prime \prime }$ . Empirical, contingent claims about deployed systems: the staleness decomposition, Pro<sub>p</sub>osition 1, Pro<sub>p</sub>osition 3A (falsified b<sub>y</sub> our own data, A<sub>pp</sub>endix A.12), and ever<sub>y</sub> cross-s<sub>y</sub>stem number in §6. Two consequences follow. The theory does not establish that the 10+1 partition, the deca<sub>y</sub> rates, or the <sub>p</sub>i<sub>p</sub>eline are o<sub>p</sub>timal; §6.7 measures how much the <sub>p</sub>artition actuall<sub>y</sub> matters. ## 6.1 LongMemEval-S Under the Wu et al. Protocol

FR-Bank achieves 75.2% ± 0.70<sub>pp p</sub>ass@10 on the full 500-<sub>q</sub>uestion Lon<sub>g</sub>MemEval-S benchmark [1] under the exact Wu et al. (ICLR 2025) jud<sub>g</sub>e <sub>p</sub>rotocol (Table 15), to our knowled<sub>g</sub>e the state of the art under the canonical rubric and above the closest <sub>p</sub>eer-reviewed result, RMM [3] at 70.4% under a diferent judge configuration. We hold the original five-template rubric and judge model (gpt-4o-2024-08-06) fixed because subse<sub>q</sub>uent work substitutes stron<sub>g</sub>er answerers, diferent judges, and simplified prompts, rendering reported numbers mutually incomparable; methodology i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>egr</sub>it<sub>y ra</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>an</sub> th<sub>e a</sub>b<sub>so</sub>l<sub>u</sub>t<sub>e num</sub>b<sub>er</sub> i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>.</sub> O<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> 317<sub>-ques</sub>ti<sub>on ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d <sub>su</sub>b<sub>se</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e</sub> la<sub>y</sub>er is a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ate-neutral (+2.2<sub>pp</sub> net, 72.9% → 75.1%), with a −6.4<sub>pp</sub> efect on knowled<sub>g</sub>e u<sub>p</sub>date (79.5% vs. 85.9%). We re<sub>p</sub>ort that inversion as a benchmark-criterion artifact rather than a lifec<sub>y</sub>cle gain: the Wu et al. judge credits a response when superseded facts appear alongside the updated answer, so filterin<sub>g</sub> is <sub>p</sub>enalized for cleaner context (A<sub>pp</sub>endix D.2). A +6.4<sub>pp</sub> knowled<sub>g</sub>e-u<sub>p</sub>date fi<sub>g</sub>ure in the submitted version traced to no survivin<sub>g</sub> artifact and is withdrawn (A<sub>pp</sub>endix AI).

A-MEM [4] <sub>p</sub>airs the hi<sub>g</sub>hest retrieval recall (87.6% Hit@5) with the hi<sub>g</sub>hest staleness (30.4%) and worst confabulation (47.0%), so recall without lifec<sub>y</sub>cle mana<sub>g</sub>ement activel<sub>y</sub> hurts downstream; u<sub>pg</sub>radin<sub>g</sub> Mem0’s extractor to <sub>gp</sub>t-4.1-mini raises <sub>p</sub>ass rate to 67.1% (+6.1<sub>pp</sub>) but leaves AV2 and AV7 unchan<sub>g</sub>ed (A<sub>pp</sub>endix O).

## 6.4 End-to-End Response Quality

F<sub>or eac</sub>h <sub>o</sub>f 516 Lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e</sub>B<sub>enc</sub>h <sub>ques</sub>ti<sub>ons, eac</sub>h <sub>sys</sub>t<sub>em</sub>’<sub>s</sub> t<sub>op-</sub>10 <sub>re</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>eve</sub>d f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>s are prov</sub>id<sub>e</sub>d <sub>as</sub> the sole context to GPT-5.4 (tem<sub>p</sub>erature 0, max\_tokens=500) with a s<sub>y</sub>stem <sub>p</sub>rom<sub>p</sub>t restrictin<sub>g</sub> answers to the provided facts; an independent Claude Sonnet judge then classifies each response as correct, <sub>p</sub>artial, wron<sub>g</sub>, or abstain and fla<sub>g</sub>s confabulation (full methodolo<sub>gy</sub> in A<sub>pp</sub>endix N). ## 7 Discussion and Conclusion

Limitations. Five, in the order we consider them binding. (i) Human validation is absent. Every verdict here comes from an LLM jud<sub>g</sub>e; we audited it with two machine instruments (§6.7) that <sub>agree on</sub> th<sub>e</sub> di<sub>rec</sub>ti<sub>on o</sub>f it<sub>s</sub> bi<sub>as</sub> b<sub>u</sub>t <sub>no</sub>t it<sub>s magn</sub>it<sub>u</sub>d<sub>e, an</sub>d th<sub>e c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>er</sub> i<sub>s au</sub>dit<sub>e</sub>d <sub>aga</sub>i<sub>ns</sub>t LLM judges rather than humans. A stratified human-labeled slice (100 verdicts, two non-author annotators blind to system identity, prioritizing items where the machine judges disagree) is planned and has not yet been run; until it is, absolute rates are judge-relative. (ii) Benchmark–method co-design risk. We built Lifec cleBench and its attack vectors were informed b failure modes we observed i<sub>n</sub> b<sub>ase</sub>li<sub>nes.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> BEAM t<sub>rans</sub>f<sub>er</sub> b<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>s</sub> thi<sub>s r</sub>i<sub>s</sub>k <sub>w</sub>ith<sub>ou</sub>t <sub>e</sub>li<sub>m</sub>i<sub>na</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> it<sub>, an</sub>d BEAM’<sub>s runs use a</sub> diferent backbone, so they are not commensurable with our LifecycleBench tables. (iii) Comparison fairness. Competitors run in shipped defaults against a deliberately engineered FR stack. The <sub>un</sub>t<sub>ype</sub>d<sub>-arm</sub> d<sub>ecompos</sub>iti<sub>on separa</sub>t<sub>es po</sub>li<sub>cy</sub> f<sub>rom eng</sub>i<sub>neer</sub>i<sub>ng w</sub>ithi<sub>n our own s</sub>t<sub>ac</sub>k <sub>an</sub>d A<sub>ppen</sub>di<sub>x</sub> E d<sub>ocumen</sub>t<sub>s eac</sub>h <sub>ex</sub>t<sub>erna</sub>l <sub>sys</sub>t<sub>em</sub>’<sub>s s</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ura</sub>l bl<sub>oc</sub>k<sub>er,</sub> b<sub>u</sub>t <sub>a max</sub>i<sub>ma</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> t<sub>une</sub>d <sub>compe</sub>tit<sub>or was no</sub>t b<sub>u</sub>ilt<sub>.</sub> (iv) No multi-framework or multi-agent evaluation. Available evidence. The full-vs-uniform ablation (73% vs 61% <sub>p</sub>ass) and the lar<sub>g</sub>e <sub>g</sub>ains on AV1/AV2/AV4/AV7 indicate that the behavioral <sub>p</sub>artition <sub>separa</sub>t<sub>es</sub> f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>s prec</sub>i<sub>se</sub>l<sub>y w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e-po</sub>li<sub>cy var</sub>i<sub>ance</sub> i<sub>s</sub> l<sub>arges</sub>t<sub>.</sub> Th<sub>ese resu</sub>lt<sub>s are cons</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t <sub>w</sub>ith (24), thou<sub>g</sub>h we <sub>p</sub>resent it as an em<sub>p</sub>irical desi<sub>g</sub>n claim rather than an anal<sub>y</sub>tic theorem.

## A.6 Partition Separation: The Behavioral Variance Gap

Theorem 2A (Partition variance gap). We prove the weighted pairwise identity underlying Theorem 2A and plug in the numerical bound from the FR ontology.

The identity. Let $G _ { j }$ be a cognitive cell with behavioral categories of weight $w _ { c } = n _ { c }$ and total weight $\begin{array} { r } { W _ { j } = \sum _ { c \in G _ { j } } w _ { c } , } \end{array}$ , and let $\begin{array} { r } { \bar { \theta } _ { j } = W _ { j } ^ { - 1 } \sum _ { c \in G _ { j } } w _ { c } \theta _ { c } } \end{array}$ . Then

$$
\sum _ { c \in G _ { j } } w _ { c } \| \theta _ { c } - \bar { \theta } _ { j } \| ^ { 2 } = \frac { 1 } { W _ { j } } \sum _ { c _ { 1 } < c _ { 2 } \atop c _ { 1 } , c _ { 2 } \in G _ { j } } w _ { c _ { 1 } } w _ { c _ { 2 } } \| \theta _ { c _ { 1 } } - \theta _ { c _ { 2 } } \| ^ { 2 } .\tag{26}
$$

Proof. Theorem 4 (Calibration feasibility). Fix a finite set of lifecycle requirements, each of the form “candidate � must outrank candidate $e ^ { \prime }$ under query $q ^ { \mathcal { \dot { \prime } } } \left( \dot { a } \right.$ reservation constraint when � is the durable fact, a suppression constraint when $e ^ { \prime }$ is stale). Under the log-score of Consequence $^ { l , }$ each requirement is a linear inequality in the per-category parameters $\theta = \{ \alpha _ { c } , \lambda _ { c } , F _ { c } \} _ { c \in C }$ , so the feasible set $\Theta _ { m }$ is a convex polyhedron. Let $L _ { \alpha } \dot { = } \left\{ \theta \in \mathbb { R } ^ { | \theta | } : \alpha _ { c } = \alpha \forall c \right\}$ be the global-� slice. Then feasibility is not preserved under restriction to $L _ { \alpha } \colon \Theta _ { m } \neq \varnothing$ while $\Theta _ { m } \cap L _ { \alpha } = \varnothing$ whenever some category pair $( c , c ^ { \prime } )$ carries a preservation bound $\alpha _ { c } ^ { \mathrm { m a x } }$ below a suppression bound $\alpha _ { c ^ { \prime } } ^ { \mathrm { m i n } }$ . Empirically this holds on 99.8% ofthe 1,566 measured cross-pairs, and on all 45 populated cells ofthe category polytope.

## A.13 Anticipatory Activation and Why Age Alone Cannot Express It

Proposition 2. No monotone nonincreasing function of edge age can represent anticipatory activation.

Proof. Suppose for contradiction that there exists a nonincreasing function $g : \mathbb { R } _ { \geq 0 } $ R <sub>suc</sub>h th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>e</sub>d<sub>ge re</sub>l<sub>evance can</sub> b<sub>e wr</sub>itt<sub>en as</sub> $u ( t ) = g ( t - t _ { e } )$ <sub>, w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $t _ { e }$ i<sub>s crea</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> ti<sub>me.</sub> T<sub>a</sub>k<sub>e</sub> ti<sub>mes</sub> $t _ { 1 } < t _ { 2 } < h$ <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> ℎ i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e even</sub>t ti<sub>me.</sub> B<sub>ecause</sub> $t _ { 1 } - t _ { e } < t _ { 2 } - t _ { e }$ <sub>an</sub>d $g$ i<sub>s non</sub>i<sub>ncreas</sub>i<sub>ng,</sub>

$$
u ( t _ { 1 } ) = g ( t _ { 1 } - t _ { e } ) \geq g ( t _ { 2 } - t _ { e } ) = u ( t _ { 2 } ) .\tag{58}
$$

But anticipatory activation requires relevance to increase as the event approaches from the left:

$$
u ( t _ { 1 } ) < u ( t _ { 2 } ) \qquad \mathrm { f o r } t _ { 1 } < t _ { 2 } < h .\tag{59}
$$

Equations (58) and (59) are contradictor<sub>y</sub>. Therefore no monotone nonincreasin<sub>g</sub> function of a<sub>g</sub>e <sub>a</sub>l<sub>one</sub> <sub>can</sub> <sub>represen</sub>t <sub>an</sub>ti<sub>c</sub>i<sub>pa</sub>t<sub>ory</sub> <sub>ac</sub>ti<sub>va</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> □

Why the FR kernel works. For $V _ { c } ( t , h ) = \exp [ - \nu _ { c } ( h - t ) _ { + } ] \ \exp [ - \mu _ { c } ( t - h ) _ { + } ]$ <sub>w</sub>ith $\mu _ { c } \gg \nu _ { c } \mathrm { : }$ if $t < \dot { h } , V _ { c } ( t , h ) = \exp [ - \nu _ { c } ( h - t ) ]$ <sub>,</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith d<sub>er</sub>i<sub>va</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> $\nu _ { c } \exp [ - \nu _ { c } ( h - t ) ] > 0 ,$ <sub>, so re</sub>l<sub>evance</sub> i<sub>ncreases as</sub> th<sub>e even</sub>t <sub>approac</sub>h<sub>es;</sub> if $t > h , V _ { c } ( t , h ) = \exp [ - \mu _ { c } ( t - h ) ]$ <sub>, w</sub>ith d<sub>er</sub>i<sub>va</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> $- \mu _ { c } \exp [ - \mu _ { c } ( t - h ) ] < 0$ <sub>so</sub> <sub>re</sub>l<sub>evance</sub> d<sub>ecreases</sub> <sub>a</sub>ft<sub>er</sub> <sub>exp</sub>i<sub>ry.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> FR k<sub>erne</sub>l h<sub>as</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>qua</sub>lit<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sub>correc</sub>t <sub>s</sub>h<sub>ape</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>an</sub> <sub>age-on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sup>d</sup>eca<sub>y</sub> cannot re<sub>p</sub>ro<sup>d</sup>uce.

## A.14 Proof of Lemma 1 (Harmonic-Mean Decay)

L<sub>e</sub>t $\tau _ { c } = 1 / \lambda _ { c }$ d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>pers</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t<sub>ence</sub> ti<sub>mesca</sub>l<sub>e</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>ca</sub>t<sub>egory</sub> <sub>�.</sub> A<sub>ssume</sub> <sub>so</sub>ft <sub>mem</sub>b<sub>ers</sub>hi<sub>ps</sub> $w _ { c } ( e ) \geq 0$ <sub>sa</sub>ti<sub>s</sub>f<sub>y</sub> $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { c } w _ { c } ( e ) = 1 } \end{array}$ <sub>an</sub>d th<sub>a</sub>t ti<sub>mesca</sub>l<sub>es</sub> <sub>average</sub> li<sub>near</sub>l<sub>y:</sub>

$$
\tau _ { \mathrm { e f f } } ( e ) = \sum _ { c } w _ { c } ( e ) \tau _ { c } .\tag{60}
$$

S<sub>u</sub>b<sub>s</sub>tit<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> $\tau _ { c } = 1 / \lambda _ { c }$ <sub>g</sub><sup>i</sup>ves $\begin{array} { r } { \tau _ { \mathrm { e f f } } ( e ) = \sum _ { c } w _ { c } ( e ) / \lambda _ { c } } \end{array}$ <sub>.</sub> Si<sub>nce</sub> $\lambda _ { \mathrm { e f f } } ( e ) = 1 / \tau _ { \mathrm { e f f } } ( e )$

$$
\lambda _ { \mathrm { e f f } } ( e ) = \left( \sum _ { c } \frac { w _ { c } ( e ) } { \lambda _ { c } } \right) ^ { - 1 } .\tag{61}
$$

Thi<sub>s</sub> i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> h<sub>armon</sub>i<sub>c mean.</sub>

Numerical example. For 30% Identity and 70% Logistical membership,

$$
\lambda _ { \mathrm { e f f } } = \left( \frac { 0 . 3 } { 0 . 0 0 1 5 } + \frac { 0 . 7 } { 0 . 0 0 8 0 } \right) ^ { - 1 } \approx 0 . 0 0 3 4 8 / \mathrm { h r } .
$$

Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>correspon</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> h<sub>a</sub>lf<sub>-</sub>lif<sub>e</sub> i<sub>s</sub> $t _ { 1 / 2 } = \ln 2 / \lambda _ { \mathrm { e f f } }$ ≈ 199.3 hr ≈ 8.3 da<sub>y</sub>s.

Soft membership in practice. On the full FR-Bank corpus (12,968 active entries across 40 persona banks), 66.8% of entries have <sub>p</sub>rimar<sub>y</sub> cate<sub>g</sub>or<sub>y</sub> wei<sub>g</sub>ht below 0.90 (28.7% below 0.80), and the mean <sub>num</sub>b<sub>er o</sub>f <sub>ca</sub>t<sub>e or</sub>i<sub>es w</sub>ith <sub>we</sub>i ht $> 0 . 0 5$ <sub>per en</sub>t<sub>ry</sub> i<sub>s</sub> 2<sub>.</sub>0<sub>.</sub> H<sub>armon</sub>i<sub>c-mean</sub> d<sub>ecay</sub> th<sub>ere</sub>f<sub>ore</sub> fi<sub>res on</sub> the majority of the corpus, not as a theoretical edge case. The most frequent co-occurrence pairs are Intellectual Interests ↔ Projects & Endeavors (635 entries), Health & Wellbeing ↔ Obligations (438), and Projects & Endeavors ↔ Relational Bonds (422), reflectin<sub>g g</sub>enuine behavioral ambi<sub>g</sub>uit<sub>y</sub>: a research project involves both intellectual engagement and deliverable-tracking, while a chronic h<sub>ea</sub>lth <sub>con</sub>diti<sub>on genera</sub>t<sub>es</sub> b<sub>o</sub>th <sub>ongo</sub>i<sub>ng s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e an</sub>d ti<sub>me-</sub>b<sub>oun</sub>d <sub>me</sub>di<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>o</sub>bli<sub>ga</sub>ti<sub>ons.</sub>

## Discussion. Four observations from the audit:

1. Context matters. A decontextualized audit (fact text only, no conversation) yields $\kappa = + 0 . 5 8$ (vs. +0.67 with context), confirmin<sub>g</sub> that behavioral classification re<sub>q</sub>uires conversational context.

T<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> 10<sub>:</sub> P<sub>er-ca</sub>t<sub>egory c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>er</sub> P/R/F1 f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e</sub> h<sub>e</sub>ld<sub>-ou</sub>t <sub>au</sub>dit $( n \ = \ 2 0 0 ;$ majority vote of three independent judges as ground truth, restricted to facts with a majority verdict, $n _ { \mathrm { e f f } } = 1 8 8 )$ . Macro-avera<sub>g</sub>ed F1 = 0.595; wei<sub>g</sub>hted F1 = 0.647; overall accurac<sub>y</sub> 66.5%.
<table><tr><td>Category</td><td>n</td><td>Precision</td><td>Recall</td><td>F1</td></tr><tr><td>RELATIONAL_BONDS</td><td>31</td><td>0.935</td><td>0.784</td><td>0.853</td></tr><tr><td>PREFERENCES_HABITS</td><td>20</td><td>0.850</td><td>0.739</td><td>0.791</td></tr><tr><td>HEALTH_WELLBEING</td><td>15</td><td>0.800</td><td>0.706</td><td>0.750</td></tr><tr><td>PROJECTS_ENDEAVORS</td><td>28</td><td>0.536</td><td>0.938</td><td>0.682</td></tr><tr><td>FINANCIAL_MATERIAL</td><td>10</td><td>0.900</td><td>0.529</td><td>0.667</td></tr><tr><td>HOBBIES_RECREATION</td><td>13</td><td>0.769</td><td>0.588</td><td>0.667</td></tr><tr><td>IDENTITY_SELF_CONCEPT</td><td>16</td><td>0.875</td><td>0.424</td><td>0.571</td></tr><tr><td>LOGISTICAL_CONTEXT</td><td>18</td><td>0.611</td><td>0.524</td><td>0.564</td></tr><tr><td>OBLIGATIONS</td><td>31</td><td>0.387</td><td>0.857</td><td>0.533</td></tr><tr><td>INTELLECTUAL_INTERESTS</td><td>12</td><td>0.333</td><td>0.800</td><td>0.471</td></tr><tr><td>OTHER</td><td>6</td><td>0.000</td><td>0.000</td><td>0.000</td></tr></table>

2. Disagreements are concentrated and explicable. OBLIGATIONS vs. LOGISTI-CAL\_CONTEXT reflects the classifier’s deliberate event-time routin<sub>g</sub> (facts with date anchors route to OBLIGATIONS for antici<sub>p</sub>ator<sub>y</sub> activation). PROJECTS\_ENDEAVORS vs. HOB-BIES\_RECREATION reflects the DELIVERABLE TEST override (§3). These are <sub>p</sub>olic<sub>y</sub> choices, not errors.

3. INTELLECTUAL\_INTERESTS over-assignment. Low agreement (33.3%) with full context <sub>sugges</sub>t<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>er may over-ass</sub>i<sub>gn</sub> thi<sub>s ca</sub>t<sub>egory;</sub> ti<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>en</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> thi<sub>s</sub> b<sub>oun</sub>d<sub>ary</sub> i<sub>s</sub> id<sub>en</sub>tifi<sub>e</sub>d <sub>as</sub> f<sub>u</sub>t<sub>ure wor</sub>k<sub>.</sub>

4. Errors concentrate where lifecycle policies are most similar. ## B.5 Safety and Robustness

Prompt injection via memory. The ingestion classifier detects instructional content and quarantines it. Hi<sub>g</sub>h-sensitivit<sub>y</sub> cate<sub>g</sub>ories (Health, Financial) fla<sub>g</sub> facts for reconfirmation if inconsistent with established patterns. Confidence degradation. Low-confidence supersessions surface both fact with ambiguity noted. Selective forgetting. Deletion requests provide clean, verifiable removal with <sub>au</sub>dit t<sub>ra</sub>il<sub>.</sub>

## C.2 On Synthetic Generation

LifecycleBench conversations are synthetically generated—as are those of every major memory benchmark in the literature. Lon<sub>g</sub>MemEval [1] <sub>g</sub>enerates its multi-session conversations with GPT-4; LoCoMo [13] constructs dialo<sub>g</sub>ues s<sub>y</sub>ntheticall<sub>y</sub> and validates with human annotation; no <sub>p</sub>ublished b<sub>enc</sub>h<sub>mar</sub>k <sub>uses</sub> <sub>na</sub>t<sub>ura</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> <sub>occurr</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>mu</sub>lti<sub>-sess</sub>i<sub>on</sub> <sub>persona</sub>l <sub>conversa</sub>ti<sub>ons,</sub> b<sub>ecause</sub> <sub>suc</sub>h d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a</sub> <sub>canno</sub>t b<sub>e co</sub>ll<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>a</sub>t <sub>sca</sub>l<sub>e w</sub>ith<sub>ou</sub>t <sub>pro</sub>hibiti<sub>ve pr</sub>i<sub>vacy an</sub>d IRB <sub>cons</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>s.</sub> Th<sub>e re</sub>l<sub>evan</sub>t <sub>ques</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> i<sub>s</sub> therefore not whether a benchmark is synthetic, but whether its synthetic design enables controlled evaluation of the target phenomenon. LifecycleBench’s contribution is precisely this control: each <sub>persona</sub> i<sub>s a</sub> f<sub>u</sub>ll<sub>y spec</sub>ifi<sub>e</sub>d t<sub>empora</sub>l <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e mac</sub>hi<sub>ne w</sub>ith <sub>exp</sub>li<sub>c</sub>it <sub>groun</sub>d<sub>-</sub>t<sub>ru</sub>th <sub>supersess</sub>i<sub>on c</sub>h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>ns,</sub> <sub>exp</sub>i<sub>ry</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es, an</sub>d <sub>re</sub>t<sub>rac</sub>ti<sub>on even</sub>t<sub>s, ena</sub>bli<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>rs</sub>t b<sub>enc</sub>h<sub>mar</sub>k <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> th<sub>e correc</sub>t <sub>answer</sub> d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>s</sub> <sub>on</sub> lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e managemen</sub>t <sub>ra</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>an re</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>eva</sub>l <sub>reca</sub>ll <sub>a</sub>l<sub>one.</sub> N<sub>o na</sub>t<sub>ura</sub>li<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>c</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t <sub>can prov</sub>id<sub>e</sub> thi<sub>s</sub> b<sub>ecause groun</sub>d<sub>-</sub>t<sub>ru</sub>th t<sub>empora</sub>l <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e—w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h f<sub>ac</sub>t <sub>superse</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h<sub>, w</sub>h<sub>en an o</sub>bli<sub>ga</sub>ti<sub>on exp</sub>i<sub>re</sub>d<sub>,</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>a</sub>t l<sub>anguage</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>user</sub> <sub>use</sub>d t<sub>o</sub> <sub>re</sub>t<sub>rac</sub>t<sub>—</sub>i<sub>s</sub> <sub>uno</sub>b<sub>serva</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith<sub>ou</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>ll<sub>e</sub>d d<sub>es</sub>i<sub>gn.</sub>

## C.4 Scale and Coverage

Corpus statistics. The 40 ersona banks contain 18,936 total entries (12,968 active, 5,968 inactive). Of i<sub>nac</sub>ti<sub>ve en</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>es,</sub> 5<sub>,</sub>780 <sub>were</sub> d<sub>eac</sub>ti<sub>va</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d b<sub>y supersess</sub>i<sub>on,</sub> 13 b<sub>y exp</sub>li<sub>c</sub>it <sub>re</sub>t<sub>rac</sub>ti<sub>on, an</sub>d 175 <sub>are</sub> otherwise unclassified. Across 12,460 distinct slot ke<sub>y</sub>s, 18.6% have been u<sub>p</sub>dated at least once (chain len<sub>g</sub>th > 1); the lon<sub>g</sub>est chains reach 45–65 versions for hi<sub>g</sub>h-churn attributes (e.<sub>g</sub>., evolvin<sub>g</sub> hobbies, current cit<sub>y</sub>, work schedule, famil<sub>y</sub> relationshi<sub>p</sub>s). Each <sub>p</sub>ersona s<sub>p</sub>ans exactl<sub>y</sub> 35 sessions over a mean of 17.4 simulated months (ran<sub>g</sub>e 14.95–18.53), at 14.5–36.9 entries <sub>p</sub>er month. The OTHER cate<sub>g</sub>or<sub>y</sub> accounts for 0.12% of entries (16 of 12,968), confirmin<sub>g</sub> that the 10-cate<sub>g</sub>or<sub>y</sub> covera<sub>g</sub>e is near-exhaustive. The three most frequent cate<sub>g</sub>ories are Projects & Endeavors (20.7%), Relational Bonds (16.9%), and Obli<sub>g</sub>ations (14.2%).

## C.5 Evaluation Metrics

(1) AV-s<sub>p</sub>ecific <sub>p</sub>ass rate: does the to<sub>p</sub>-� retrieval set su<sub>pp</sub>ort the correct answer? (2) Staleness <sub>p</sub>enalt<sub>y</sub>: does it contain outdated ed<sub>g</sub>es? An LLM jud<sub>g</sub>e (Claude Sonnet) evaluates each (question, ed<sub>g</sub>e) <sub>p</sub>air. To<sub>p</sub>-10 jud<sub>g</sub>e window (s<sub>p</sub>lit: correct-answer in to<sub>p</sub>-10, staleness in to<sub>p</sub>-5).

Generator-judge independence. LifecycleBench conversations are generated by Claude Opus 4.6 with extended thinking, while the retrieval and E2E judges use Claude Sonnet. Although both are Anthropic models, the judge evaluates factual correctness against structured ground-truth YAML (su<sub>p</sub>ersession chains, ex<sub>p</sub>ir<sub>y</sub> dates, retraction events), not <sub>g</sub>eneration qualit<sub>y</sub>. The jud<sub>g</sub>e’s task is to d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>erm</sub>i<sub>ne</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>e</sub>th<sub>er</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>spec</sub>ifi<sub>c</sub> f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>ua</sub>l <sub>answer</sub> <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> YAML <sub>spec</sub>ifi<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>on;</sub> <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l<sub>-</sub>f<sub>am</sub>il<sub>y</sub> <sub>a</sub>fi<sub>n</sub>it<sub>y</sub> d<sub>oes no</sub>t <sub>a</sub>d<sub>van</sub>t<sub>a e or</sub> di<sub>sa</sub>d<sub>van</sub>t<sub>a e an memor s s</sub>t<sub>em, s</sub>i<sub>nce a</sub>ll fi<sub>ve s s</sub>t<sub>ems are eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d b th<sub>e</sub> same judge on the same questions. Cross-generator validation on Kimi K2.5 (Moonshot, open-weight; A<sub>pp</sub>endix AB) <sub>p</sub>reserves the confabulation hierarch<sub>y</sub> exactl<sub>y</sub>, <sub>p</sub>rovidin<sub>g</sub> evidence that the rankin<sub>g</sub> is judge-invariant.

Why not MRR? Replication of Liu et al. [15] on Claude Sonnet (2026) at 50-document scale <sub>s</sub>h<sub>ows no s</sub>i<sub>gn</sub>ifi<sub>can</sub>t <sub>pos</sub>iti<sub>ona</sub>l <sub>e</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>t $( r = - 0 . 0 0 9 , p = 0 . 6 5 )$ <sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l <sub>processes a</sub>ll <sub>re</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>eve</sub>d f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>s</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>near-equa</sub>l <sub>a</sub>tt<sub>en</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> MRR i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>ere</sub>f<sub>ore</sub> <sub>un</sub>i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>orma</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> f<sub>or</sub> d<sub>owns</sub>t<sub>ream</sub> <sub>accuracy.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>pos</sub>iti<sub>ona</sub>l <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>l<sub>eness e</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>t <sub>opera</sub>t<sub>es</sub> th<sub>roug</sub>h <sub>con</sub>fli<sub>c</sub>t <sub>reso</sub>l<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on un</sub>d<sub>er con</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>di<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>on, no</sub>t <sub>u</sub>tili<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> failure. We nevertheless re<sub>p</sub>ort MRR in A<sub>pp</sub>endix D for com<sub>p</sub>arabilit<sub>y</sub> with <sub>p</sub>rior work (Ze<sub>p</sub>/Gra<sub>p</sub>hiti and Memor<sub>y</sub>-R1 re<sub>p</sub>ort MRR as a <sub>p</sub>rimar<sub>y</sub> metric), while usin<sub>g p</sub>ass rate as our <sub>p</sub>rimar<sub>y</sub> evaluation <sub>me</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c</sub> th<sub>roug</sub>h<sub>ou</sub>t<sub>.</sub>

Exhaustive failure-mode coverage. The 9 attack vectors enumerate the exhaustive set of temporal-<sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> f<sub>a</sub>il<sub>ure mo</sub>d<sub>es</sub> f<sub>or pers</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t <sub>persona</sub>l <sub>memory ra</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>an</sub> FR<sub>-spec</sub>ifi<sub>c capa</sub>biliti<sub>es.</sub> A f<sub>ac</sub>t <sub>can</sub> b<sub>e</sub> re<sub>p</sub>laced (AV1, AV4), an event can ex<sub>p</sub>ire (AV2), a statement can be retracted (AV7), information can be <sub>p</sub>artiall<sub>y</sub> u<sub>p</sub>dated (AV9), stable facts can be buried under conversation volume (AV3), facts can require cross-ed<sub>g</sub>e a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ation (AV5), sessions can contradict each other (AV6), and s<sub>p</sub>ecific numeric values can be lost in abstraction (AV8). An<sub>y</sub> inde<sub>p</sub>endentl<sub>y</sub> constructed lifec<sub>y</sub>cle benchmark would <sub>nee</sub>d t<sub>o cover</sub> th<sub>e same</sub> f<sub>a</sub>il<sub>ure mo</sub>d<sub>es,</sub> b<sub>ecause</sub> th<sub>ese are proper</sub>ti<sub>es o</sub>f th<sub>e pro</sub>bl<sub>em</sub> d<sub>oma</sub>i<sub>n, no</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f <sub>any</sub> <sub>par</sub>ti<sub>cu</sub>l<sub>ar so</sub>l<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub>

FR does not dominate all vectors. MemoryOS leads AV6 (Cross-Session Contradiction) at 73% <sub>versus</sub> FR<sub>-</sub>B<sub>an</sub>k’<sub>s</sub> 64%<sub>, an</sub>d l<sub>ea</sub>d<sub>s</sub> AV7 <sub>on</sub> th<sub>e re</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>eva</sub>l <sub>me</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c.</sub> FR<sub>-</sub>G<sub>rap</sub>hiti<sub>, no</sub>t FR<sub>-</sub>B<sub>an</sub>k<sub>,</sub> l<sub>ea</sub>d<sub>s</sub> AV2 and AV8. FR-Bank achieves onl<sub>y</sub> 5% retrieval <sub>p</sub>ass on AV7 (Selective For<sub>g</sub>ettin<sub>g</sub>) and near-zero correct rates on AV5 and AV6 end-to-end (Table 42). If the benchmark were desi<sub>g</sub>ned to favor FR, these <sub>g</sub>a<sub>p</sub>s would not exist. (The submitted version stated that Memor<sub>y</sub>-R1 leads AV3 at 94%; that <sub>co</sub>m<sub>pa</sub>ri<sub>so</sub>n <sub>was</sub> <sub>aga</sub>in<sub>s</sub>t FR<sub>-</sub>Gr<sub>ap</sub>hiti’<sub>s</sub> 78%<sub>.</sub> FR<sub>-</sub>B<sub>a</sub>nk l<sub>ea</sub>d<sub>s</sub> AV3 <sub>a</sub>t 97%<sub>,</sub> <sub>a</sub>nd M<sub>e</sub>m<sub>o</sub>r<sub>y-</sub>R1 l<sub>ea</sub>d<sub>s</sub> n<sub>o</sub> attack vector.)

## D Extended Experimental Results

## D.1 LongMemEval Detailed Results

Primary result: LongMemEval-S 500-question canonical benchmark. The full 500-question Lon<sub>g</sub>MemEval-S setu<sub>p</sub> of Wu et al. (ICLR 2025) is our headline evaluation. We rerun the full suite 10 times under identical confi<sub>g</sub>uration (tem<sub>p</sub>erature 0 throu<sub>g</sub>hout, same retrieval <sub>p</sub>i<sub>p</sub>eline and to<sub>p</sub>-10 facts per question) to characterize judge non-determinism. All 10 runs land at or above the locked baseline of 74.2%, with a mean of 75.2% and a maximum of 76.6% (Table 15). Per-cate<sub>g</sub>or<sub>y</sub> dis<sub>p</sub>ersion is small exce<sub>p</sub>t for sin<sub>g</sub>le-session-<sub>p</sub>reference, where the small denominator (�=30) inflates stdev.

## D.2 Lifecycle Contribution Decomposition

LME-S lifecycle decomposition (317-question matched subset). On the 317 Lon MemEval-S <sub>ques</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> f<sub>or w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h b<sub>o</sub>th lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e an</sub>d <sub>no-</sub>lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e con</sub>fi<sub>gura</sub>ti<sub>ons were eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>on</sub> id<sub>en</sub>ti<sub>ca</sub>l b<sub>an</sub>k<sub>s,</sub> lifec<sub>y</sub>cle contributes +2.2<sub>pp</sub> net (72.9% → 75.1%). The contribution concentrates on the <sub>q</sub>uestion <sub>ca</sub>t<sub>egor</sub>i<sub>es w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> t<sub>empora</sub>l<sub>-s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e managemen</sub>t i<sub>s requ</sub>i<sub>re</sub>d<sub>:</sub> k<sub>now</sub>l<sub>e</sub>d<sub>ge-up</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e an</sub>d <sub>mu</sub>lti<sub>-sess</sub>i<sub>on</sub> <sub>ques</sub>ti<sub>ons, w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> th<sub>e</sub> lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e</sub> l<sub>ayer removes con</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>di<sub>c</sub>t<sub>ory or superse</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>s</sub> b<sub>e</sub>f<sub>ore re</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>eva</sub>l<sub>; on</sub> th<sub>e</sub> remainin<sub>g</sub> task t<sub>yp</sub>es lifec<sub>y</sub>cle is a<sub>pp</sub>roximatel<sub>y</sub> neutral. The knowled<sub>g</sub>e-u<sub>p</sub>date inversion (−6.4<sub>pp</sub>) reveals a benchmark criterion limitation: the Wu et al. judge marks responses correct when both the <sub>o</sub>ld <sub>an</sub>d <sub>up</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>answer are presen</sub>t<sub>, so</sub> lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e</sub> filt<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>correc</sub>tl<sub>y removes superse</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>s</sub> i<sub>s</sub> <sub>pena</sub>li<sub>ze</sub>d f<sub>or</sub> <sub>pro</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>eaner</sub> <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t<sub>.</sub> Thi<sub>s</sub> <sub>cr</sub>it<sub>er</sub>i<sub>on</sub> i<sub>mp</sub>li<sub>c</sub>itl<sub>y</sub> t<sub>rea</sub>t<sub>s</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>l<sub>e</sub> f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>s</sub> <sub>as</sub> h<sub>arm</sub>l<sub>ess—an</sub> <sub>assump</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> di<sub>rec</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>di<sub>c</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> <sub>our</sub> <sub>en</sub>d<sub>-</sub>t<sub>o-en</sub>d <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>l<sub>e</sub> <sub>re</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>eva</sub>l <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t <sub>pro</sub>d<sub>uces</sub> 72–78% confabulation (Table 40). ## D.5 Infrastructure Findings

World-knowledge contamination. 86% of extracted edges were world knowledge—reduced to 1.4% with detection filterin<sub>g</sub> across ∼12,700 ed<sub>g</sub>es.

Numeric preservation. Graphiti’s deduplication pipeline systematically loses numeric values th<sub>roug</sub>h d<sub>e</sub>d<sub>up reso</sub>l<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on an</sub>d b<sub>u</sub>lk d<sub>e</sub>d<sub>up canon</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>se</sub>l<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> N<sub>umer</sub>i<sub>c-pre</sub>f<sub>erence reso</sub>l<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> increased numeric ed<sub>g</sub>e densit<sub>y</sub> from ∼5% to 20.1%.

Per-source score normalization. Prior to normalization, 99.1% of top-5 results came from Graphiti’s <sub>seman</sub>ti<sub>c</sub> <sub>searc</sub>h <sub>a</sub>l<sub>one.</sub> Aft<sub>er</sub> <sub>per-source</sub> <sub>norma</sub>li<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> <sub>a</sub>ll <sub>re</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>eva</sub>l <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>eg</sub>i<sub>es</sub> b<sub>ecame</sub> <sub>mean</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>f<sub>u</sub>l <sub>con</sub>t<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>t<sub>ors.</sub>

## D.11 Failure Taxonomy

Automated classification of 90 failures at 20-<sub>p</sub>ersona scale: Retrieval Miss 43 (47.8%), Extraction Missin<sub>g</sub> 25 (27.8%), Stale Dominance 12 (13.3%), Extraction Weak 7 (7.8%), Cross-Cate<sub>g</sub>or<sub>y</sub> 3 (3.3%). AV7 failures are 100% extraction missin<sub>g</sub>. AV3 failures are 90% retrieval miss.

## D.12 Reasoning Does Not Close the Architectural Gap

Queries with stale facts consume +9.2 reasonin<sub>g</sub> tokens on avera<sub>g</sub>e (+11%) as the model detects <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>di<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>ons.</sub>

## D.13 The Retrieval Metric Paradox

For AV7 questions where Memor<sub>y</sub>OS retrieval <sub>p</sub>asses, 22/23 cases (96%) had hierarchical summarization replace specific retracted plans with generic summaries. The judge marks these as passing b<sub>ecause no s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>l<sub>e</sub> f<sub>ac</sub>t i<sub>s presen</sub>t<sub>—</sub>b<sub>u</sub>t <sub>no use</sub>f<sub>u</sub>l f<sub>ac</sub>t i<sub>s presen</sub>t <sub>e</sub>ith<sub>er.</sub> E<sub>n</sub>d<sub>-</sub>t<sub>o-en</sub>d<sub>:</sub> M<sub>emory</sub>OS 7<sub>.</sub>5% <sub>correc</sub>t <sub>on</sub> AV7 <sub>versus</sub> FR<sub>-</sub>B<sub>an</sub>k’<sub>s</sub> 37<sub>.</sub>5%<sub>.</sub>

## E Detailed Gap Analysis Per System

## E.1 Zep/Graphiti (2025): Eight Identified Gaps

Zep achieves 94.8% on DMR and up to 71.2% on LongMemEval. Gap 1: No forgetting mechanism. Facts either exist or get explicitly superseded. Gap 2: No behavioral awareness. Scheduling details and identity traits are structurally identical edges. Gap 3: Purely reactive retrieval. No anticipatory capability. Gap 4: No session initialization intelligence. Gap 5: Communities are structural, not behavioral. Gap 6: Binary supersession under ambiguity. Gap 7: Noisy retrieval on certain query types. Gap 8: Self-identified ontology gap.

## E.2 MemoryBank (AAAI 2024): Five Gaps

Gap 1: Single uniform forgetting curve. Gap 2: No graph structure. Gap 3: Single temporal reference frame. Gap 4: No behavioral categorization. Gap 5: Limited evaluation.

## E.3 A-MEM (NeurIPS 2025): Four Gaps

Gap 1: No temporal dynamics. Gap 2: No forgetting. Gap 3: Content-driven, not behavior-driven evolution. Gap 4: LLM-heavy at runtime.

## E.4 MemGPT / Letta (2024): Three Gaps

Gap 1: The LLM is the policy—expensive, opaque, and unreliable. Gap 2: No internal structure in archival. Gap 3: No temporal decay, supersession, or event-time validity.

## E.5 Mem0 (2025): Four Gaps (Empirically Validated)

Gap 1: No structured lifecycle. Validated: AV1 staleness is 49%. Gap 2: No category awareness. Validated: 87% on AV3 but 44% on AV1 and 5% on AV7. Gap 3: No event-time expiry. Validated: AV2 staleness is 33%. Gap 4: Default storage does not persist between processes.

## E.6 MemoryOS (2025): Three Gaps

Gap 1: Cognitive ontology, not behavioral. Validated: 70.5% retrieval pass but 13.2% E2E correct.   
Gap 2: No per-category decay conditioning. Gap 3: Fixed hierarchy with 30× ingestion compute.

## E.7 Memory-R1 (2025): Four Gaps

Gap 1: Learned policy is opaque. Gap 2: Requires massive interaction data. Gap 3: No behavioral structure in action space. Gap 4: No multi-reference-frame temporal sensitivity.

## E.8 MIRIX (2025) and FluxMem (2026)

MIRIX: cognitive types; uniform temporal treatment; LLM-heavy. FluxMem: adapts structure not <sub>po</sub>li<sub>cy; or</sub>th<sub>ogona</sub>l<sub>.</sub>

Even if both are addressed, the Answer A<sub>g</sub>ent’s <sub>p</sub>ure-cosine retrieval (:139–148) <sub>p</sub>rovides no scorin<sub>g</sub> h<sub>oo</sub>k <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere a s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e mas</sub>k <sub>cou</sub>ld b<sub>e app</sub>li<sub>e</sub>d<sub>—a</sub> thi<sub>r</sub>d<sub>,</sub> i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>t f<sub>or</sub>k <sub>po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>.</sub>

Per-fact identit is reserved in STM (short\_term.py:16–23) and within MTM sessions, but the MTM→LTM consolidation ste<sub>p</sub> (memoryos.py:162–200) extracts a monolithic <sub>p</sub>rofile strin<sub>g p</sub>lus a flat knowled<sub>g</sub>e de<sub>q</sub>ue (long\_term.py:17–18); neither carries back a link to the <sub>or</sub>i<sub>g</sub>i<sub>na</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> MTM <sub>page.</sub> Aft<sub>er conso</sub>lid<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> t<sub>wo con</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>di<sub>c</sub>t<sub>ory</sub> LTM f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>s coex</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>no mec</sub>h<sub>an</sub>i<sub>sm</sub> for fact-level invalidation (long\_term.py:69–73). ## F Slot-Key Supersession: Extended Examples

Slot keys are normalized (subject, attribute) pairs for categories with replace semantics:

• “User likes <sub>p</sub>izza” → (user, food\_preference)

• “User likes sushi” → high-confidence supersession

• “User lives in Istanbul” → (user, current\_city)

• “User is thinkin<sub>g</sub> about movin<sub>g</sub> to London” → low-confidence (both survive)

• “User moved to London” → high-confidence supersession of Istanbul

For categories with accumulate semantics (Identity, Relational), supersession requires explicit <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>di<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>on w</sub>ith hi<sub>g</sub>h <sub>con</sub>fid<sub>ence.</sub>

## G Multi-Reference-Frame Temporal Sensitivity

Three temporal clocks: Absolute time (calendar clock): governs Logistical decay and deadline proximity. Relative time (session clock): time since last session; governs Relational Bonds and Hobbies (dormancy detection). Conversational frequency: how often a fact appears across sessions; <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>u</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es</sub> b<sub>ase</sub> d<sub>ecay ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub> d<sub>ownwar</sub>d<sub>.</sub>

Per-cate<sub>g</sub>or<sub>y</sub> routin<sub>g</sub>: Lo<sub>g</sub>istical → <sub>p</sub>rimaril<sub>y</sub> absolute time; Relational → session <sub>g</sub>a<sub>p</sub>s; Obli<sub>g</sub>ations → deadline <sub>p</sub>roximit<sub>y</sub>; Identit<sub>y</sub> → near-zero sensitivit<sub>y</sub>; Hobbies → relative time; Preferences → <sub>a</sub>b<sub>so</sub>l<sub>u</sub>t<sub>e</sub> ti<sub>me.</sub>

## H Numeric Preservation: Detailed Mechanisms

Loss Point 1: Deduplication Resolution. When the dedup LLM marks a new edge as duplicate of an existing edge, the system keeps the old (often generic) edge. Loss Point 2: Bulk Deduplication Canonical Selection. Canonical selected by smallest UUID—arbitrary.

Multi-stage numeric rescue: (1) Pool-stage exemption from expiry filter for monetary/quantity ed<sub>g</sub>es. (2) Pool-sta<sub>g</sub>e exem<sub>p</sub>tion from su<sub>p</sub>ersession filter. (3) Rerankin<sub>g</sub>-sta<sub>g</sub>e floor <sub>g</sub>uarantee (0.95× semantic score for numeric ed<sub>g</sub>es with <sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>hiti\_score ≥ 0.85). Combined: AV8 recovered from 81% t<sub>o</sub> 94%<sub>.</sub>

## J Phase-by-Phase Development and Results

## J.1 Phase 2: Initial Validation (8 personas, 112 questions)

## J.2 Between-Phase Improvements

Phase 2 → 3: Scale 8→20 personas; per-source score normalization; cross-category supersession;   
world-knowled<sub>g</sub>e filter (86%→1.4%).

Phase 3 → 4: Scale 20→40 personas; retraction extraction; AV8 numeric rescue; date-aware expiry filter; <sub>p</sub>arallel evaluation (12× s<sub>p</sub>eedu<sub>p</sub>).

Phase 4 → 5b: Multi-stage numeric rescue; backward-looking expiry bypass; date resolver future-<sub>p</sub>re<sup>f</sup>erence <sub>g</sub>uar<sup>d</sup>.

Phase 5b → 5c: Identity/health supplementary extraction: 2,996 candidates → 388 retained (13%).

## K Detailed Findings from LifecycleBench Evaluation

Backward-looking query detection. Keyword patterns (“before switching”, “used to”) bypass the <sub>supersess</sub>i<sub>on</sub> filt<sub>er,</sub> <sub>recover</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> 3 <sub>o</sub>th<sub>erw</sub>i<sub>se</sub> <sub>unreac</sub>h<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> <sub>ques</sub>ti<sub>ons.</sub>

Retraction filtering. AV7 staleness drops from 25% to 12% with the retraction filter scanning for markers (“<sub>p</sub>lan is dead”, “scra<sub>pp</sub>ed”).

Date-aware expiry filtering. Parses ISO dates, month+day patterns, contextual months, and relative markers. Cate<sub>g</sub>or<sub>y</sub>-<sub>g</sub>uarded (Lo<sub>g</sub>istical, Obli<sub>g</sub>ations, Health onl<sub>y</sub>) with 14-da<sub>y</sub> bufer.

Category-specific �. With uniform � = 0.3, AV8 drops to 12.5%. Category-specific: Financial at 0<sub>.</sub>05<sub>,</sub> L<sub>og</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>a</sub>t 0<sub>.</sub>40<sub>.</sub>

Semantic floors. Near-perfect semantic matches for stable categories are not buried by zero activation.   
Financial floor 0.97; Lo<sub>g</sub>istical floor 0.00. Hit@1: 19%→31%.

Pass-by-absence. Both systems exhibit this on AV2. FR: 50 cases (expiry filter); Mem0: 29 (extraction failure).

AV3 tradeof decomposition. 77% extraction misses, 12% decay-killed, 12% partial.

## N End-to-End Evaluation Details

## N.1 Methodology

For each of 516 <sub>q</sub>uestions, each s<sub>y</sub>stem’s to<sub>p</sub>-10 facts are fed to GPT-5.4 (gpt-5.4 [22]; tem<sub>p</sub>erature=0, max\_tokens=500) with a s<sub>y</sub>stem <sub>p</sub>rom<sub>p</sub>t restrictin<sub>g</sub> answers to <sub>p</sub>rovided facts. Claude Sonnet (claude-sonnet-4-6) jud<sub>g</sub>es correctness (CORRECT, PARTIAL, WRONG, ABSTAIN) and confabulation. No Anthro<sub>p</sub>ic models for <sub>g</sub>eneration. Memor<sub>y p</sub>i<sub>p</sub>elines (FR-Bank in<sub>g</sub>esti<sub>on</sub>/di<sub>s</sub>till<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> M<sub>em</sub>0 d<sub>e</sub>f<sub>au</sub>lt <sub>ex</sub>t<sub>rac</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> M<sub>emory-</sub>R1 <sub>ex</sub>t<sub>rac</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> A<sub>-</sub>MEM <sub>evo</sub>l<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>on,</sub> M<sub>em</sub>0<sub>-m</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i ablation) use gpt-4.1-mini (versioned: gpt-4.1-mini-2025-04-14); Mem0’s default extraction uses gpt-4.1-nano; Kimi K2.5 cross-<sub>g</sub>enerator validation uses kimi-k2.5 via the Moonshot API. The Lon<sub>g</sub>MemEval-S jud<sub>g</sub>e under the Wu et al. <sub>p</sub>rotocol is gpt-4o-2024-08-06, as re<sub>p</sub>orted in §6.

Memory-R1 validates that learned operations cannot substitute for missing mechanisms. Memor<sub>y</sub>-R1’s RL-trainable action space {ADD, UPDATE, DELETE, NOOP} achieves strong retrieval qualit<sub>y</sub> (MRR 0.816, above FR-Gra<sub>p</sub>hiti’s 0.478 thou<sub>g</sub>h below FR-Bank’s 0.830) and <sub>p</sub>erforms well on stable identit<sub>y</sub> retrieval (AV3: 94%, above FR-Gra<sub>p</sub>hiti’s 78% but below FR-Bank’s 97%). However, the action s<sub>p</sub>ace contains no event-time ex<sub>p</sub>ir<sub>y</sub> o<sub>p</sub>eration (AV2: FR-Gra<sub>p</sub>hiti 93%, FR-Bank 88.0% vs MR1 75%, 25% staleness), no structured retraction mechanism (AV7: FR-Gra<sub>p</sub>hiti 40% vs MR1 15%, 42% staleness), and no numeric <sub>p</sub>reservation <sub>p</sub>i<sub>p</sub>eline (AV8: FR-Gra<sub>p</sub>hiti 94%, FR-Bank 84.1% vs MR1 57%). These are vocabular<sub>y g</sub>a<sub>p</sub>s in the action s<sub>p</sub>ace—no reward si<sub>g</sub>nal can i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>uce an ac</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t d<sub>oes no</sub>t <sub>ex</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t<sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e approac</sub>h<sub>es are comp</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>ary:</sub> FR’<sub>s on</sub>t<sub>o</sub>l<sub>ogy prov</sub>id<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ura</sub>l <sub>pr</sub>i<sub>or</sub> <sub>over</sub> <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e</sub> <sub>po</sub>li<sub>cy</sub> <sub>app</sub>li<sub>es;</sub> RL <sub>op</sub>ti<sub>m</sub>i<sub>zes</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>app</sub>l<sub>y</sub> it<sub>.</sub>

## O Mem0 Comparison Methodology

Mem0 v1.0.5 (A<sub>p</sub>ache 2.0), 100% default confi<sub>g</sub>uration: <sub>gp</sub>t-4.1-nano extraction, text-embeddin<sub>g</sub>-3- small embeddin<sub>g</sub>s, Qdrant vector store. The onl<sub>y</sub> non-default settin<sub>g</sub> was enablin<sub>g</sub> disk persistence, <sub>as</sub> M<sub>em</sub>0’<sub>s</sub> d<sub>e</sub>f<sub>au</sub>lt i<sub>n-memory</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>orage</sub> d<sub>oes</sub> <sub>no</sub>t <sub>surv</sub>i<sub>ve</sub> b<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ween</sub> <sub>process</sub> i<sub>nvoca</sub>ti<sub>ons.</sub> All 40 <sub>personas</sub> (1,400 sessions) were in<sub>g</sub>ested throu<sub>g</sub>h m.add() API with raw conversation turns, and all 516 questions evaluated with the same Claude Sonnet LLM judge. Default extraction model produced fr<sub>eque</sub>nt JSON <sub>pa</sub>r<sub>se e</sub>rr<sub>o</sub>r<sub>s</sub> d<sub>u</sub>rin<sub>g</sub> UPDATE <sub>a</sub>nd DELETE <sub>ope</sub>r<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n<sub>s, caus</sub>in<sub>g so</sub>m<sub>e</sub> lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e up</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es</sub> t<sub>o</sub> f<sub>a</sub>il <sub>s</sub>il<sub>en</sub>tl<sub>y.</sub>

## Z FR-Bank Architecture and Methodology

FR-Bank re<sub>p</sub>laces Gra<sub>p</sub>hiti with a standalone embeddin<sub>g</sub>-indexed memor<sub>y</sub> bank (∼780 lines). Ingestion: One GPT-4.1-mini call per turn for combined extraction and classification. Nearduplicates (cosine > 0.95) skipped; numeric facts exempt. Supersession: Instance-qualified slot-keys. Replace-semantics categories: threshold 0.8. Accumulate-semantics: threshold 0.95. Retrieval: (1) Cosine to<sub>p</sub>-60, (2) BM25 to<sub>p</sub>-20, (3) cate<sub>g</sub>or<sub>y</sub>-forced to<sub>p</sub>-20, (4) mer<sub>g</sub>e/dedu<sub>p</sub>, (5) staleness-aware distillation to<sub>p</sub>-20, (6) lifec<sub>y</sub>cle filters, (7) blended scorin<sub>g</sub>, (8) to<sub>p</sub>-10 out<sub>p</sub>ut.

Ingestion statistics. 18,936 total entries; 12,968 active (31.5% deactivated). Average 324.2 active <sub>p</sub>er <sub>p</sub>ersona. (The submitted version re<sub>p</sub>orted 5,344 / 3,892 / 133.6, a stale <sub>p</sub>roof-of-conce<sub>p</sub>t sna<sub>p</sub>shot; the values here are verified a<sub>g</sub>ainst the released lifecycle\_banks/.)

## AA LongMemEval FR-Bank Evaluation Methodology

P<sub>er-ques</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> b<sub>an</sub>k<sub>s are crea</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d f<sub>or</sub> th<sub>e orac</sub>l<sub>e</sub> f<sub>orma</sub>t<sub>.</sub> B<sub>o</sub>th <sub>user an</sub>d <sub>ass</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>t t<sub>urns are</sub> i<sub>nges</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d<sub>.</sub> A <sub>raw-</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t BM25 f<sub>a</sub>llb<sub>ac</sub>k h<sub>an</sub>dl<sub>es zero-ex</sub>t<sub>rac</sub>ti<sub>on ques</sub>ti<sub>ons.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>u</sub>ll <sub>re</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>eva</sub>l <sub>p</sub>i<sub>pe</sub>li<sub>ne runs w</sub>ith identical <sub>p</sub>arameters to Lifec<sub>y</sub>cleBench. Answer <sub>g</sub>eneration uses gpt-4o-mini (tem<sub>p</sub>erature 0, max\_tokens 300) over the to<sub>p</sub>-10 retrieved facts; fact extraction uses gpt-4.1-mini.

Per-question banks are the standard oracle protocol. The per-question bank structure is not an FR-Bank desi<sub>g</sub>n choice: it is the evaluation <sub>p</sub>rotocol defined b<sub>y</sub> Lon<sub>g</sub>MemEval [1] and matched b<sub>y</sub> LoCoMo [13], both of which score each <sub>q</sub>uestion a<sub>g</sub>ainst its own conversation ha<sub>y</sub>stack rather than a<sub>g</sub>ainst a sin<sub>g</sub>le <sub>p</sub>ersistent memor<sub>y</sub> store. Ever<sub>y p</sub>eer-reviewed Lon<sub>g</sub>MemEval result (Wu et al. ICLR 2025; RMM ACL 2025 [3]) is <sub>p</sub>roduced under the same <sub>p</sub>er-<sub>q</sub>uestion setu<sub>p</sub>, so FR-Bank’s 75.2% on L<sub>ong</sub>M<sub>em</sub>E<sub>va</sub>l<sub>-</sub>S <sub>an</sub>d 79<sub>.</sub>5% <sub>on</sub> th<sub>e</sub> 234<sub>-ques</sub>ti<sub>on orac</sub>l<sub>e</sub> POC <sub>su</sub>b<sub>se</sub>t <sub>are</sub> di<sub>rec</sub>tl<sub>y compara</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>ose</sub> <sub>re</sub>f<sub>erences.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>u</sub>ll<sub>y pers</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>on se</sub>tti<sub>ng—one memory s</sub>t<sub>ore accumu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> 35 <sub>mu</sub>lti<sub>-sess</sub>i<sub>on</sub> <sub>conversa</sub>ti<sub>ons per persona an</sub>d 516 lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e-sens</sub>iti<sub>ve quer</sub>i<sub>es aga</sub>i<sub>ns</sub>t th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>same s</sub>t<sub>ore—</sub>i<sub>s exac</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>reg</sub>i<sub>me measure</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> Lif<sub>ecyc</sub>l<sub>e</sub>B<sub>enc</sub>h<sub>, w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> FR<sub>-</sub>B<sub>an</sub>k <sub>reac</sub>h<sub>es</sub> 76<sub>.</sub>9% <sub>pass w</sub>ith 15<sub>.</sub>5% <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>l<sub>eness.</sub>

Judge protocol (Wu et al. verbatim). All reported LongMemEval-S numbers use gpt-4o-2024-08-06 as the jud<sub>g</sub>e at tem<sub>p</sub>erature 0, with the <sub>q</sub>uestion-t<sub>yp</sub>e routin<sub>g</sub> and <sub>p</sub>rom<sub>p</sub>t tem<sub>p</sub>lates taken verbatim from Fi<sub>g</sub>ure 10 of Wu et al. (ICLR 2025). Five tem<sub>p</sub>lates are used, one <sub>p</sub>er <sup>task</sup> <sup>t</sup>yp<sup>e:</sup>

• Default (single-session-user<sub>,</sub> single-session-assistant<sub>,</sub> multi-session): <sub>mar</sub>k<sub>s</sub> “<sub>yes</sub>” <sub>w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> th<sub>e mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l <sub>response con</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>ns</sub> th<sub>e correc</sub>t <sub>answer or a</sub>ll i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erme</sub>di<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>eps;</sub> “<sub>no</sub>” <sub>w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> <sub>on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>su</sub>b<sub>se</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> <sub>requ</sub>i<sub>re</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>orma</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> i<sub>s</sub> <sub>presen</sub>t<sub>.</sub>

• temporal-reasoning: default tem<sub>p</sub>late <sub>p</sub>lus ex<sub>p</sub>licit of-b<sub>y</sub>-one tolerance—res<sub>p</sub>onses <sub>pre</sub>di<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>ng, e.g.,</sub> 19 d<sub>ays w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> th<sub>e answer</sub> i<sub>s</sub> 18 <sub>are mar</sub>k<sub>e</sub>d <sub>correc</sub>t<sub>.</sub>

• knowledge-update: res<sub>p</sub>onses that contain <sub>p</sub>rior information alon<sub>g</sub>side the u<sub>p</sub>dated answer <sub>are</sub> <sub>mar</sub>k<sub>e</sub>d <sub>correc</sub>t <sub>prov</sub>id<sub>e</sub>d th<sub>e</sub> <sub>up</sub>d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>answer</sub> i<sub>s</sub> <sub>presen</sub>t<sub>.</sub>

• single-session-preference: rubric-based <sub>p</sub>artial credit—the res<sub>p</sub>onse need not cover <sub>every ru</sub>b<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t b<sub>u</sub>t <sub>mus</sub>t <sub>correc</sub>tl<sub>y reca</sub>ll <sub>an</sub>d <sub>u</sub>tili<sub>se</sub> th<sub>e user</sub>’<sub>s persona</sub>l i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>orma</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub>

• abstention (routed when the <sub>q</sub>uestion id contains \_abs): marks “<sub>y</sub>es” onl<sub>y</sub> if the model <sub>correc</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> id<sub>en</sub>tifi<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e ques</sub>ti<sub>on as unanswera</sub>bl<sub>e.</sub>

Each jud<sub>g</sub>e call is deterministic (max\_tokens=10, tem<sub>p</sub>erature 0) and the verdict is <sub>p</sub>arsed from the <sub>presence</sub> <sub>o</sub>f “<sub>yes</sub>” i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>response.</sub> A<sub>cross</sub> 10 id<sub>en</sub>ti<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>reruns,</sub> t<sub>o</sub>t<sub>a</sub>l <sub>pass</sub> <sub>ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>moves</sub> <sub>w</sub>ithi<sub>n</sub> 74<sub>.</sub>4<sub>–</sub>76<sub>.</sub>6% (stdev 0.70<sub>pp</sub>; Table 15), which we attribute to residual non-determinism in the hosted jud<sub>g</sub>e end<sub>p</sub>oint <sub>ra</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>an</sub> t<sub>o our p</sub>i<sub>pe</sub>li<sub>ne.</sub>

The evaluate\_qa.py routine in the oficial LongMemEval release uses th<sub>ese</sub> <sub>same</sub> fi<sub>ve</sub> t<sub>emp</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>oses</sub>t <sub>peer-rev</sub>i<sub>ewe</sub>d L<sub>ong</sub>M<sub>em</sub>E<sub>va</sub>l <sub>resu</sub>lt <sub>pr</sub>i<sub>or</sub> t<sub>o</sub> thi<sub>s</sub> <sub>wor</sub>k<sub>,</sub> RMM (Tan et al., ACL 2025), substitutes Gemini-1.5-Pro for gpt-4o-2024-08-06 and re<sub>p</sub>laces the five tem<sub>p</sub>lates with a sin<sub>g</sub>le <sub>g</sub>eneric <sub>p</sub>rom<sub>p</sub>t (their A<sub>pp</sub>endix D.3). Because neither the jud<sub>g</sub>e model nor th<sub>e ru</sub>b<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>es,</sub> RMM’<sub>s</sub> 70<sub>.</sub>4% <sub>an</sub>d <sub>our</sub> 75<sub>.</sub>2% <sub>are no</sub>t <sub>measure</sub>d <sub>on</sub> th<sub>e same ax</sub>i<sub>s; we repor</sub>t th<sub>em</sub> <sub>s</sub>id<sub>e-</sub>b<sub>y-s</sub>id<sub>e</sub> <sub>on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> t<sub>o</sub> fl<sub>ag</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t FR<sub>-</sub>B<sub>an</sub>k i<sub>s,</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>our</sub> k<sub>now</sub>l<sub>e</sub>d<sub>ge,</sub> th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>rs</sub>t <sub>peer-rev</sub>i<sub>ewe</sub>d <sub>sys</sub>t<sub>em</sub> <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>un</sub>d<sub>er</sub> th<sub>e exac</sub>t W<sub>u e</sub>t <sub>a</sub>l<sub>. ru</sub>b<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c.</sub>

Behavioral diference. Kimi K2.5 systematically prefers answering over abstaining: correct rate i<sub>ncreases</sub> b<sub>y</sub> +1<sub>.</sub>9 t<sub>o</sub> +6<sub>.</sub>8<sub>pp across a</sub>ll <sub>sys</sub>t<sub>ems w</sub>hil<sub>e a</sub>b<sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub> d<sub>ecreases</sub> b<sub>y</sub> 13<sub>.</sub>0 t<sub>o</sub> 14<sub>.</sub>4<sub>pp.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>a</sub>b<sub>s</sub>t<sub>en</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> d<sub>rop</sub> i<sub>s</sub> <sub>muc</sub>h l<sub>arger</sub> th<sub>an</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>correc</sub>t<sub>-ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>ga</sub>i<sub>n</sub> b<sub>ecause</sub> <sub>mos</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> <sub>ex</sub>t<sub>ra</sub> <sub>answer</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>capac</sub>it<sub>y</sub> <sub>conver</sub>t<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o wrong answers.</sub> Thi<sub>s</sub> i<sub>s a un</sub>if<sub>orm</sub> b<sub>e</sub>h<sub>av</sub>i<sub>ora</sub>l <sub>s</sub>hift<sub>, an</sub>d th<sub>e re</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> hi<sub>erarc</sub>h<sub>y</sub> i<sub>s preserve</sub>d<sub>.</sub>

This rules out three <sub>p</sub>otential confounds: (a) GPT-5.4-s<sub>p</sub>ecific quirks drivin<sub>g</sub> the result, (b) shared O<sub>p</sub>enAI trainin<sub>g</sub> data biases, (c) self-<sub>p</sub>reference efects from <sub>g</sub>enerator-jud<sub>g</sub>e ali<sub>g</sub>nment. Retrieval <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t <sub>qua</sub>lit<sub>y</sub> d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>erm</sub>i<sub>nes</sub> d<sub>owns</sub>t<sub>ream response qua</sub>lit<sub>y</sub> i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e genera</sub>t<sub>or.</sub>

Methodology. For each of 516 questions across 4 systems, the same retrieved top-10 facts used in the GPT-5.4 evaluation were fed to Kimi K2.5 (hosted via Moonshot’s API at https://api.moonshot.ai/v1). Persona-name resolution was added to the <sub>p</sub>rom<sub>p</sub>t. The same Claude Sonnet judge evaluated both generators. Total Kimi K2.5 API cost for 2,064 generations: under \$10.

Recovery of Mem0 E2E data. The original Mem0 E2E results were spread across three git commits <sub>an</sub>d <sub>par</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> <sub>overwr</sub>itt<sub>en.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>comp</sub>l<sub>e</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>merge</sub>d d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t <sub>was</sub> <sub>recons</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d f<sub>or</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>cross-genera</sub>t<sub>or</sub> <sub>va</sub>lid<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub>

On AV2 the correct-rate <sub>g</sub>a<sub>p</sub> is even shar<sub>p</sub>er: FR-Bank 19.7% versus the next-best 7.0%, a 2.8× lead driven b<sub>y</sub> event-time ex<sub>p</sub>ir<sub>y</sub> filterin<sub>g</sub>.

MemoryOS on Kimi: generator-dependent confab reduction, but the retrieval-metric paradox persists. On GPT-5.4, MemoryOS sits at 40.8% confabulation. On Kimi K2.5, MemoryOS drops to 29.2%—an 11.6<sub>pp</sub> reduction concentratin<sub>g</sub> on AV2 (67.5%→43.2%), AV7 (81.2%→50.0%), and AV1 (43.2%→31.7%). Manual ins<sub>p</sub>ection su<sub>gg</sub>ests Kimi is more willin<sub>g</sub> to hed<sub>g</sub>e when <sub>p</sub>resented with <sub>g</sub>eneric summaries. The LifecycleBench result validates the thesis—through precision, not recall. At 40-persona <sub>sca</sub>l<sub>e,</sub> FR <sub>ac</sub>hi<sub>eves</sub> 73% <sub>pass w</sub>ith 8% <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>l<sub>eness versus</sub> b<sub>ase</sub>li<sub>ne</sub>’<sub>s</sub> 61%/18%<sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> 4<sub>-con</sub>fi<sub>g a</sub>bl<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> cleanl<sub>y</sub> decom<sub>p</sub>oses contributions. Five-<sub>p</sub>hase scalin<sub>g</sub> validation (8→40 <sub>p</sub>ersonas) confirms <sub>g</sub>ains are <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e.</sub>

End-to-end evaluation closes the causal loop. The retrieval-to-response chain is now empirically validated: lifec<sub>y</sub>cle mana<sub>g</sub>ement → cleaner context → fewer confabulations. The confabulation <sub>g</sub>a<sub>p</sub> (−22.7<sub>pp</sub> over answered queries, −19.2<sub>pp</sub> over all queries) is lar<sub>g</sub>er than the <sub>p</sub>ass rate <sub>g</sub>a<sub>p</sub> (+16<sub>pp</sub>), confirming that lifecycle management’s primary value is preventing wrong answers, not just finding ri<sub>g</sub>ht ones. The safe res<sub>p</sub>onse rate (correct + abstain) ca<sub>p</sub>tures this: FR-Bank 73.3% and FR-Gra<sub>p</sub>hiti 66<sub>.</sub>9%<sub>, aga</sub>i<sub>ns</sub>t M<sub>em</sub>0’<sub>s</sub> 47<sub>.</sub>3%<sub>.</sub> B<sub>o</sub>th FR <sub>var</sub>i<sub>an</sub>t<sub>s pro</sub>d<sub>uce responses</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t d<sub>o no</sub>t <sub>m</sub>i<sub>s</sub>l<sub>ea</sub>d <sub>users on</sub> <sub>roug</sub>hl<sub>y</sub> t<sub>wo-</sub>thi<sub>r</sub>d<sub>s or more o</sub>f <sub>quer</sub>i<sub>es.</sub>

Cross-system comparison confirms the thesis at the field level. Against Mem0, Memory-R1, and M<sub>emory</sub>OS<sub>—represen</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> fl<sub>a</sub>t <sub>vec</sub>t<sub>or s</sub>t<sub>ores,</sub> RL<sub>-</sub>l<sub>earne</sub>d <sub>opera</sub>ti<sub>ons, an</sub>d <sub>cogn</sub>iti<sub>ve</sub> hi<sub>erarc</sub>hi<sub>es—</sub>FR<sub>-</sub> Bank achieves the hi<sub>g</sub>hest <sub>p</sub>ass rate (76.9%) and lowest confabulation (22.4%). The five-s<sub>y</sub>stem com<sub>p</sub>arison reveals a clean hierarch<sub>y</sub>: no lifec<sub>y</sub>cle mana<sub>g</sub>ement (Mem0) < learned o<sub>p</sub>erations (Memor<sub>y</sub> R1) < co<sub>g</sub>nitive hierarch<sub>y</sub> (Memor<sub>y</sub>OS) < desi<sub>g</sub>ned lifec<sub>y</sub>cle <sub>p</sub>olicies (FR-Bank). Memor<sub>y</sub>OS’s com<sub>p</sub>etitive retrieval metrics (70.5% <sub>p</sub>ass) mask a downstream failure: onl<sub>y</sub> 13.2% correct rate, <sub>con</sub>fi<sub>rm</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>cogn</sub>iti<sub>ve</sub> <sub>on</sub>t<sub>o</sub>l<sub>og</sub>i<sub>es</sub> d<sub>o</sub> <sub>no</sub>t <sub>preserve</sub> f<sub>ac</sub>t<sub>-</sub>l<sub>eve</sub>l <sub>granu</sub>l<sub>ar</sub>it<sub>y.</sub>

The retrieval metric paradox reveals a methodological gap. ## AE.1 The Benchmark, and Why It Answers the Co-Design Objection

BEAM [14] contains 100 coherent conversations and exactl<sub>y</sub> 2,000 <sub>p</sub>robin<sub>g q</sub>uestions—two <sub>p</sub>er <sub>conversa</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> f<sub>or eac</sub>h <sub>o</sub>f t<sub>en memory a</sub>biliti<sub>es—across con</sub>t<sub>ex</sub>t ti<sub>ers</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> 128K t<sub>o</sub> 10M t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>ens.</sub> It <sub>was</sub> constructed inde<sub>p</sub>endentl<sub>y</sub> of this work and is human-validated. Three of its abilities (contradiction resolution, event orderin<sub>g</sub>, instruction followin<sub>g</sub>) were newl<sub>y</sub> introduced b<sub>y</sub> its authors, so the<sub>y</sub> <sub>canno</sub>t d<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ve</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> FR’<sub>s</sub> d<sub>es</sub>i <sub>n or</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e</sub> b<sub>ase</sub>li<sub>ne</sub> f<sub>a</sub>il<sub>ure mo</sub>d<sub>es</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>orme</sub>d Lif<sub>ec c</sub>l<sub>e</sub>B<sub>enc</sub>h’ attack vectors. ## AE.2 Standing Disclosures

Two disclosures bind ever<sub>y</sub> number in this a<sub>pp</sub>endix and in §6.6.

Backbone quarantine. These runs use a diferent generator backbone from the canonical Lifecy-<sub>c</sub>l<sub>e</sub>B<sub>enc</sub>h <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>arms</sub> <sub>are</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erna</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> <sub>compara</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>one</sub> <sub>ano</sub>th<sub>er</sub> <sub>un</sub>d<sub>er</sub> <sub>an</sub> id<sub>en</sub>ti<sub>ca</sub>l h<sub>arness,</sub> but the absolute values are not commensurable with the LifecycleBench tables elsewhere in this paper, <sub>an</sub>d <sub>we</sub> d<sub>o no</sub>t <sub>poo</sub>l th<sub>em.</sub>

Judge asymmetry. The judge used here has perfect abstention recall (15/15 on a bridge sample a<sub>g</sub>ainst our frozen API jud<sub>g</sub>e) but over-abstains relative to it (<sub>p</sub>recision 15/18). ## AG Validation of the End-to-End Judge

Every verdict in this paper is produced by an LLM judge, so we audited that judge with two independent <sub>mac</sub>hi<sub>ne</sub> i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>rumen</sub>t<sub>s</sub> b<sub>e</sub>f<sub>ore</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>a</sub>i<sub>m</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>any</sub>thi<sub>ng</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> it<sub>s</sub> <sub>ou</sub>t<sub>pu</sub>t<sub>.</sub> B<sub>o</sub>th <sub>were</sub> <sub>pre-reg</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t<sub>ere</sub>d <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>exp</sub>li<sub>c</sub>it <sub>pre</sub>di<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>ons;</sub> th<sub>ree o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>our pre</sub>di<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>ons were re</sub>f<sub>u</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d<sub>, an</sub>d <sub>we re</sub>l<sub>ease</sub> th<sub>em a</sub>l<sub>ongs</sub>id<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e resu</sub>lt<sub>s.</sub>

## AG.1 Six-Model Cross-Family Panel

One hundred stratified verdicts were re-judged by six models spanning five model families, each <sub>rece</sub>i<sub>v</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>a</sub> b<sub>y</sub>t<sub>e-</sub>id<sub>en</sub>ti<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>ru</sub>b<sub>r</sub>i<sub>c</sub> <sub>an</sub>d th<sub>e</sub> <sub>groun</sub>d<sub>-</sub>t<sub>ru</sub>th <sub>ev</sub>id<sub>ence,</sub> i<sub>n</sub> f<sub>res</sub>h <sub>sess</sub>i<sub>ons</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>memory</sub> disabled. The panel agrees with itself substantially more than with our pinned judge: Fleiss � = 0.834 <sub>across</sub> th<sub>e s</sub>i<sub>x pane</sub>li<sub>s</sub>t<sub>s an</sub>d 0<sub>.</sub>796 <sub>across a</sub>ll <sub>seven ra</sub>t<sub>ers, w</sub>ith <sub>a me</sub>di<sub>an pa</sub>i<sub>rw</sub>i<sub>se � o</sub>f 0<sub>.</sub>814 <sub>among</sub> panelists but 0.715 between panelists and the pinned judge. Fifteen of the 21 pairs fall outside [0.60, 0.75].

The disagreement is not difuse but localized. Against the panel majority, the pinned judge’s correct, wrong and abstain verdicts are stable (23/24, 19/20 and 32/32 res<sub>p</sub>ectivel<sub>y</sub>), while of 24 <sub>p</sub>inned partial verdicts the panel majority moves 19 elsewhere—13 to correct and 6 to abstain. Inspection of those 19 shows none contains an outdated or wrong indicator; all 19 are merely incomplete. The pinned judge is therefore using partial to mean “incomplete” where our rubric reserves it for “contaminated.” One item is a 3–3 tie with no panel majority and is reported as its own case rather th<sub>an</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ld<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> <sub>an</sub> <sub>agreemen</sub>t ti<sub>er.</sub>
